[E_{2}]=\zeta(\alpha_{2}+\operatorname{image}(j^{*}))=\zeta(\alpha_{0}+\operatorname{image}(j^{*}))+\zeta(\alpha_{1}+\operatorname{image}(j^{*}))=[E_{0}]+[E_{1}].

Thus, the sum in
\operatorname{Ext}^{\prime}(C,A)
is the Baer sum, as claimed. The zero
element is
\zeta(0)
, which is the pushout of the extension

Q=(I_{C}^{2}\xrightarrow{j}I_{C}\xrightarrow{q}C)
along the map
0\colon I^{2}_{C}\to A
. If we
use the notation of Proposition
8.30
in this
context we have

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\{(0,-j(y))\;|\;y\in I_{C}^{2}\}=0\oplus I_{C}^{2}\leq A\oplus I_{C}

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{A\oplus I_{C}}{R}=\frac{A\oplus I_{C}}{0\oplus I_{C}^{2}}=A\oplus(I_{C}/I^{2}_{C})\simeq A\oplus C

\displaystyle i^{\prime}(a)

\displaystyle=(a,0)

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(a,c)

\displaystyle=c.

Thus,
0_{*}Q
is just the obvious split extension of
C
by
A
.
∎

We now present a result that will help us relate homology groups to
cohomology groups. There are very standard theorems that deduce
information about cohomology from information about homology. To go
in the opposite direction we need the following proposition, which is
less well-known.

Proposition 8.34
.

Suppose that
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z})
and
\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})
are finitely generated.
Then
A
is finitely generated.

The proof will follow after some lemmas.

Lemma 8.35
.

Suppose that
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z})
is finitely generated. Then
A=B\oplus F

for some subgroups
B
and
F
such that
F
is free and finitely
generated, and
\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathbb{Z})=0
.

Proof.

Choose maps
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{r}\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}
that generate
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z})
, and
define
f\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}^{r}
by
f(a)=(f_{1}(a),\dotsc,f_{r}(a))
. Now
f(A)
is
a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
, so it is free, with basis

f(a_{1}),\dotsc,f(a_{s})
say. Put
B=\ker(f)
, and let
F\leq A
be
the subgroup generated by
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{s}
. We find that

f\colon F\to f(A)\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{s}
is an isomorphism, and thus that
A=B\oplus F
.
Consider a homomorphism
g\colon B\to\mathbb{Z}
. Then the composite

A=B\oplus F\xrightarrow{\text{proj}}B\xrightarrow{g}\mathbb{Z}

is an element of the group
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z})
, which is generated by the
maps
f_{i}
, but
f_{i}(B)=0
, so we see that
g=0
. This proves
that
\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathbb{Z})=0
.
∎

Lemma 8.36
.

Suppose that
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})=0
. Then
A=0
.

Proof.

Corollary
8.24
implies that
\operatorname{Ext}(\operatorname{tors}(A),\mathbb{Z})=0
, so
Proposition
8.26
gives
\operatorname{tors}(A)=0
, so
A
is torsion
free. We thus have short exact sequences
A\xrightarrow{n}A\to A/n
for all

n>0
, and using the resulting six term sequences we deduce that

\operatorname{Ext}(A/n,\mathbb{Z})=0
. As
A/n
is torsion we can use
Proposition
8.26
again to see that
A/n=0
, so
n.1_{A}
is
surjective. It is also injective because
\operatorname{tors}(A)=0
, so it is an
isomorphism. We can thus make
A
into a vector space over
{\mathbb{Q}}
by
the rule
(m/n).a=(n.1_{A})^{-1}(ma)
. Linear algebra therefore tells
us that either
A
is zero, or it has
{\mathbb{Q}}
as a summand. In the
latter case
\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})
would contain the uncountable group

\operatorname{Ext}({\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{Z})
as a summand, which is impossible as
\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})=0
.
We therefore have
A=0
as claimed.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.34
.

Using Lemma
8.35
we reduce to the case where

\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z})=0
. We next claim that there are only finitely many
primes
p
for which
A[p]\neq 0
. Indeed, for any such
p
we see
(by linear algebra over the field
\mathbb{Z}/p
) that
\operatorname{Hom}(A[p],\mathbb{Z}/p)\neq 0
. If there are infinitely many such primes, we deduce that the
group
P=\prod_{p}\operatorname{Hom}(A[p],\mathbb{Z}/p)
is uncountable, which is impossible
as Proposition
8.26
tells us that
P
is a quotient of
the finitely generated group
\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})
. We can thus choose
p

such that
A[p]=0
, so we have a short exact sequence

A\xrightarrow{p}A\xrightarrow{}A/p
. Proposition
8.23
then tells us
that multiplication by
p
is surjective on
\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})
. By the
structure theory of finitely generated groups, we see that

\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})
must be finite, of order
n
say, and that
n
must be
coprime to
p
.

Next we have short exact sequences
A[n]\xrightarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}nA
and

nA\xrightarrow{j}A\xrightarrow{g}A/n
, where
f(a)=na
and
g
is the quotient map.
By assumption we have
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z})=0
, and also

\operatorname{Hom}(A[n],\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{Hom}(A/n,\mathbb{Z})=0
because
\mathbb{Z}
is torsion free. From
the six term sequences we find that
\operatorname{Hom}(nA,\mathbb{Z})=0
. We also find
that
j^{*}\colon\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{Ext}(nA,\mathbb{Z})
is surjective and

f^{*}\colon\operatorname{Ext}(nA,\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})
is injective, but the composite

f^{*}j^{*}=(jf)^{*}
is just multiplication by
n
. As
n
was defined to
be the order of
\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})
we deduce that
f^{*}j^{*}=0
, which implies
that
\operatorname{Ext}(nA,\mathbb{Z})=0
. Lemma
8.36
therefore tells
us that
nA=0
, so
A
is torsion and
\operatorname{Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})=\operatorname{Hom}(A,{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})=A^{*}
.
This is finitely generated by assumption. Moreover, we have
nA=0

and therefore
nA^{*}=0
, so
A^{*}
is a finite group. It follows that

A^{**}
is finite but Remark
8.19
gives an
embedding
A\to A^{**}
so
A
is finite, as required.
∎

9.
Localisation

Definition 9.1
.

A
multiplicative set
is a set
S
of positive integers that
contains
1
and is closed under multiplication.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
A
be an abelian group, and let
S
be a multiplicative set. We
introduce an equivalence relation on the set
A\times S
by declaring
that
(a,s)\sim(b,t)
iff
bsx=atx
for some
x\in S
. We write

a/s
for the equivalence class of the pair
(a,s)
, and we write

A[S^{-1}]
for the set of equivalence classes. We make this into an
abelian group by the rule

a/s+b/t=(at+bs)/st.

Remark 9.3
.

Various checks are required to ensure that this definition is
meaningful. First, we must show that the given relation really is an
equivalence relation. It is clearly reflexive (as we can take
x=1
)
and symmetric. Suppose that
(a,s)\sim(b,t)\sim(c,u)
, so there
are elements
x,y\in S
with
atx=bsx
and
buy=cty
. Put

z=txy\in S
and note that

auz=(au)(txy)=(atx)(uy)=(bsx)(uy)=(buy)(sx)=(cty)(sx)=(cs)(txy)=csz,

so
(a,s)\sim(c,u)
, as required.

Next, we should check that addition is well-defined. More
specifically, suppose that
(a_{0},s_{0})\sim(a_{1},s_{1})
and

(b_{0},t_{0})\sim(b_{1},t_{1})
. Put
(c_{k},u_{k})=(a_{k}t_{k}+b_{k}s_{k},s_{k}t_{k})
;
we must show that
(c_{0},u_{0})\sim(c_{1},u_{1})
. By hypothesis there are
elements
x,y\in S
such that
a_{0}s_{1}x=a_{1}s_{0}x
and

b_{0}t_{1}y=b_{1}t_{0}y
. We multiply these two equations by
t_{0}t_{1}y
and

s_{0}s_{1}x
respectively, and then add them together to get

a_{0}s_{1}xt_{0}t_{1}y+b_{0}t_{1}ys_{0}s_{1}x=a_{1}s_{0}xt_{0}t_{1}y+b_{1}t_{0}ys_{0}s_{1}x,

or equivalently

(a_{0}t_{0}+b_{0}s_{0})(s_{1}t_{1})xy=(a_{1}t_{1}+b_{1}s_{1})(s_{0}t_{0})xy.

If we put
z=xy\in S
this can be rewritten as

c_{0}u_{1}z=c_{1}u_{0}z
, so
(c_{0},u_{0})\sim(c_{1},u_{1})
as required.

Finally, we should show that addition is commutative and associative,
that the element
0/1
is an additive identity, and that
(-a)/s
is
an additive inverse for
a/s
. All this is left to the reader.

Remark 9.4
.

From the definitions we see that
a/s=0
in
A[S^{-1}]
if and only
if there exists
t\in S
with
at=0
.

Remark 9.5
.

In the case
A=\mathbb{Z}
it is not hard to see that
\mathbb{Z}[S^{-1}]
can be
identified with the set
\{n/s\in{\mathbb{Q}}\;|\;n\in\mathbb{Z},\;s\in S\}
, which is a
subring of
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Remark 9.6
.

Consider the case where
A
is finite, so
A
is the direct sum of
its Sylow subgroups, say
A=A_{1}\oplus\dotsb\oplus A_{r}
with

|A_{i}|=p_{i}^{v_{i}}
for some primes
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}
and integers

v_{i}>0
. We then find that
A[S^{-1}]=\bigoplus_{k}A_{k}[S^{-1}]
.
Suppose that there exists
n\in S
that is divisible by
p_{k}
. In

A_{k}[S^{-1}]
we then have

a/s=(an^{v_{k}})/(sn^{v_{k}})=0/(sn^{v_{k}})=0
, so
A_{k}[S^{-1}]=0
. On the
other hand, if there is no such
n
then for each
s\in S
we see
that
s.1_{A_{k}}
is invertible for all
s\in S
, and using this we
will see later that
A_{k}[S^{-1}]=A_{k}
. Thus,
A[S^{-1}]
is just the
direct sum of some subset of the Sylow subgroups.

Definition 9.7
.

We use different notation for the most popular cases, as follows:

(a)

If
S=\{n^{k}\;|\;k\geq 0\}
we write
A[n^{-1}]
or

A[1/n]
for
A[S^{-1}]
.

(b)

If
p
is prime and

S=\{n>0\;|\;n\neq 0\pmod{p}\}=\mathbb{N}\setminus p\mathbb{N}
then we write
A_{(p)}

for
A[S^{-1}]
. This is called the
p
-localisation
of

A
.

(c)

If
S=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\;|\;n>0\}
then we write
A{\mathbb{Q}}
or
A_{(0)}

for
A[S^{-1}]
. This is called the
rationalisation
of
A
.

Proposition 9.8
.

Let
S
be a multiplicative set. Then any homomorphism
f\colon A\to B

gives a homomorphism
f[S^{-1}]\colon A[S^{-1}]\to B[S^{-1}]
by the rule

f[S^{-1}](a/s)=f(a)/s
. This construction gives an additive
functor, and there is a natural map
\eta\colon A\to A[S^{-1}]
given by

\eta(a)=a/1
.

Proof.

First, we see from the definitions that if
(a_{0},s_{0})\sim(a_{1},s_{1})

then
(f(a_{0}),s_{0})\sim(f(a_{1}),s_{1})
. This shows that
f[S^{-1}]
is
well-defined. It also follows directly from the definitions that it
is a homomorphism. Next, if we have maps
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}C
then

(gf)[S^{-1}](a/s)=gf(a)/s=g[S^{-1}](f(a)/s)=g[S^{-1}](f[S^{-1}](a/s)),

which shows that our construction is functorial. We also claim that

\eta
is natural, which means that for any
f\colon A\to B
the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[S^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[S^{-1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[S^{-1}]}

commutes. This is again straightforward.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

When there is no danger of confusion, we will just write
f
rather
than
f[S^{-1}]
for the induced map
A[S^{-1}]\to B[S^{-1}]
.

Proposition 9.10
.

If
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}C
is exact (or short exact), then so is the
localised sequence

A[S^{-1}]\xrightarrow{f[S^{-1}]}B[S^{-1}]\xrightarrow{g[S^{-1}]}C[S^{-1}].

Proof.

First, as
gf=0
and localisation is functorial we see that

g[S^{-1}]f[S^{-1}]=(gf)[S^{-1}]=0
, so

\operatorname{image}(f[S^{-1}])\leq\ker(g[S^{-1}])
. Now consider an element

b/s\in\ker(g[S^{-1}])
. We then have
g(b)/s=0/1
in
B[S^{-1}]
, or
equivalently
g(b)\,x=0
for some
x\in S
. This means that

g(xb)=0
so
xb\in\ker(g)=\operatorname{image}(f)
, so there exists
a\in A
with

f(a)=xb
. This implies that

f[S^{-1}](a/(xs))=f(a)/(xs)=xb/xs=b/s
, so

b/s\in\operatorname{image}(f[S^{-1}])
. We now see that the sequence

A[S^{-1}]\xrightarrow{f[S^{-1}]}B[S^{-1}]\xrightarrow{g[S^{-1}]}C[S^{-1}]
is
exact as claimed. Now suppose that the original sequence is short
exact. It is equivalent to say that the sequences
0\to A\to B
and

A\to B\to C
and
B\to C\to 0
are all exact, and it follows from
this that the sequences
0\to A[S^{-1}]\to B[S^{-1}]
and

A[S^{-1}]\to B[S^{-1}]\to C[S^{-1}]
and

B[S^{-1}]\to C[S^{-1}]\to 0
are also exact. We can then reassemble
these pieces to see that the sequence

A[S^{-1}]\xrightarrow{f[S^{-1}]}B[S^{-1}]\xrightarrow{g[S^{-1}]}C[S^{-1}]
is
again short exact.
∎

Proposition 9.11
.

There is a natural isomorphism
\mu\colon\mathbb{Z}[S^{-1}]\otimes A\to A[S^{-1}]

given by
\mu((n/s)\otimes a)=(na)/s
.

Proof.

First, it is straightforward to check that there is a well-defined
bilinear map
\mu_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}[S^{-1}]\times A\to A[S^{-1}]
given by

\mu_{0}(n/s,a)=(na)/s
. By the universal property of tensor products,
this gives a homomorphism
\mu\colon\mathbb{Z}[S^{-1}]\otimes A\to A[S^{-1}]
with

\mu((n/s)\otimes a)=(na)/s
. In the opposite direction, we would like
to define
\nu\colon A[S^{-1}]\to\mathbb{Z}[S^{-1}]\otimes A
by

\nu(a/s)=(1/s)\otimes a
. To see that this is well-defined, suppose
that
a/s=b/t
, so
xta=xsb
for some
x\in S
. From the definition
of tensor products, we have
mu\otimes v=u\otimes mv
in
U\otimes V
for all

u\in U
,
v\in V
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. We can apply this with

u=1/(stx)\in\mathbb{Z}[S^{-1}]
and
v=a
and
m=tx
to get

(1/s)\otimes a=(1/(stx))\otimes xta
. By a symmetrical argument, we have

(1/t)\otimes b=(1/(stx))\otimes xsb
, but
xta=xsb
so we find that

(1/s)\otimes a=(1/t)\otimes b
, as required. It is clear that

\mu\nu=1_{A[S^{-1}]}
. The other way around, we have

\nu\mu((n/s)\otimes a)=\nu((na)/s)=(1/s)\otimes na=(n/s)\otimes a.

Thus,
\nu
is inverse to
\mu
.
∎

Definition 9.12
.

Let
S
be a multiplicative set, and let
A
be an abelian group. We
say that
A
is
S
-torsion
if for all
a\in A
there exists

s\in S
with
sa=0
. We say that
A
is
S
-local
if for
each
s\in S
, the endomorphism
s.1_{A}\colon A\to A
is invertible.

Some care is needed in relating the above definition to the
traditional terminology in the most popular cases:

Definition 9.13
.

(a)

Definition
4.1
(c) is equivalent to the
following: we say that
A
is a
torsion group
if it is

S_{0}
-torsion, where
S_{0}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\;|\;n>0\}
.

(b)

We say that
A
is
rational
if it is
S_{0}
-local.
(We will see that in this case,
A
can be regarded as a vector
space over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.)

(c)

Now let
p
be a prime number. We say that
A
is

p
-torsion
if it is
p^{\mathbb{N}}
-torsion, where

p^{\mathbb{N}}=\{p^{n}\;|\;n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

(d)

However, we say that
A
is
p
-local
if it is

S_{p}
-local, where
S_{p}=\mathbb{N}\setminus p\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 9.14
.

(a)

The group
A[S^{-1}]
is always
S
-local.

(b)

The map
\eta\colon A\to A[S^{-1}]
is an isomorphism if and
only if
A
is
S
-local.

(c)

If
A
is
S
-local then it can be regarded as a module
over the ring
\mathbb{Z}[S^{-1}]\leq{\mathbb{Q}}
by the rule

(n/s).a=(s.1_{A})^{-1}(na)
.

(d)

Suppose that
f\colon A\to B
is a homomorphism, and that
B

is
S
-local. Then there is a unique homomorphism

f^{\prime}\colon A[S^{-1}]\to B
such that
f^{\prime}\circ\eta=f\colon A\to B
.

Proof.

(a)

One can check that for each
s\in S
there is a
well-defined map
d_{s}\colon A[S^{-1}]\to A[S^{-1}]
given by

d_{s}(a/t)=a/(st)
. This is inverse to
s.1_{A[S^{-1}]}
.

(b)

If
\eta
is an isomorphism then it follows from (a) that

A
is
S
-local. Conversely, if
A
is
S
-local one can check
that the formula
\zeta(a/s)=(s.1_{A})^{-1}(a)
gives a well-defined
map
\zeta\colon A[S^{-1}]\to A
, and that this is inverse to
\eta
.

(c)

First we must check that the multiplication rule is
well-defined. Suppose that
n/s=m/t
, so
ntx=msx
for some

x\in S
. As this is an equation in
\mathbb{Z}
and
x>0
it reduces to

tn=ms
. If we write
n_{A}
for
n.1_{A}
and so on, we deduce that

t_{A}n_{A}=m_{A}s_{A}\colon A\to A
. We can compose on the left by
t_{A}^{-1}

and on the right by
s_{A}^{-1}
to get
n_{A}s_{A}^{-1}=t_{A}^{-1}m_{A}
. As

s_{A}^{-1}
is a homomorphism, it commutes with multiplication by

n
, so
s_{A}^{-1}n_{A}=t_{A}^{-1}m_{A}
. This means that the definition
of multiplication is consistent. We will leave it to the reader
that it has the usual associativity and distributivity properties.

(d)

We define
f^{\prime}\colon A[S^{-1}]\to B
by

f^{\prime}(a/s)=(s.1_{B})^{-1}(f(a))
. We leave it to the reader to show
that this is well-defined and is a homomorphism. Note that

f^{\prime}(a/1)=f(a)
, so
f^{\prime}\eta=f
. If
f^{\prime\prime}
is another homomorphism
with
f^{\prime\prime}\eta=f
, we have

s_{A}(f^{\prime\prime}(a/s))=s.f^{\prime\prime}(a/s)=f^{\prime\prime}(s.(a/s))=f^{\prime\prime}(a/1)=f^{\prime\prime}(\eta(a))=f(a).

As
s_{A}
is invertible we can rewrite this as

f^{\prime\prime}(a/s)=s_{A}^{-1}(f(a))=f^{\prime}(a/s)
. As
a/s
was arbitrary this
means that
f^{\prime\prime}=f^{\prime}
, which gives that claimed uniqueness statement.

∎

Remark 9.15
.

As a consequence of (b), we can identify
A[S^{-1}]
with

A[S^{-1}][S^{-1}]
. There is a slight subtlety here: there are two
apparently different isomorphisms
A[S^{-1}]\to A[S^{-1}][S^{-1}]
,
and to keep everything straight it is necessary to prove that they
are the same. Indeed, for any
B
, we have a map

\eta_{B}\colon B\to B[S^{-1}]
. We can specialise to the case
B=A[S^{-1}]

to get a map
\eta_{A[S^{-1}]}\colon A[S^{-1}]\to A[S^{-1}][S^{-1}]
,
given by
\eta_{A[S^{-1}]}(a/s)=(a/s)/1
. Alternatively, we can
apply Proposition
9.8
to the map

\eta_{A}\colon A\to A[S^{-1}]
to get another map

\eta_{A}[S^{-1}]\colon A[S^{-1}]\to A[S^{-1}][S^{-1}]
, given by

(\eta_{A}[S^{-1}])(a/s)=(a/1)/s
. It is clear that

s.\eta_{A[S^{-1}]}(a/s)=(a/1)/1=s.(\eta_{A}[S^{-1}])(a/s),

and multiplication by
s
is an isomorphism on
A[S^{-1}][S^{-1}]
,
so
\eta_{A[S^{-1}]}=\eta_{A}[S^{-1}]
.

Proposition 9.16
.

(a)

If we have a short exact sequence
A\to B\to C
in which
two of the three terms are
S
-local, then so is the third.

(b)

Direct sums, products and retracts of
S
-local groups are

S
-local.

(c)

The kernel, cokernel and image of any homomorphism
between
S
-local groups are
S
-local.

(d)

p
-torsion groups are
p
-local.

Proof.

(a)

Note that
U
is
S
-local iff for each
n\in S
we have

U[n]=0
and
U/n=0
. Recall also that
Proposition
7.20
gives exact sequences

0\to A[n]\xrightarrow{j}B[n]\xrightarrow{q}C[n]\xrightarrow{\delta}A/n\xrightarrow{j}B/n\xrightarrow{q}C/n\to 0.

The claim follows by diagram chasing.

(b)

This is clear, because direct sums, products and retracts
of isomorphisms are isomorphisms.

(c)

Let
f\colon A\to B
be a homomorphism between
S
-local
groups. Then
\operatorname{img}(f)
is a subgroup of
B
so for
n\in S
we
have
\operatorname{img}(f)[n]\leq B[n]=0
. Similarly,
\operatorname{img}(f)
is a quotient
of
A
so
\operatorname{img}(f)/n
is a quotient of
A/n
and so is zero. It
follows that
\operatorname{img}(f)
is
S
-local. We can therefore apply (a) to
the short exact sequences
\ker(f)\to A\xrightarrow{f}\operatorname{img}(f)
and

\operatorname{img}(f)\to B\to\operatorname{cok}(f)
to see that
\ker(f)
and
\operatorname{cok}(f)
are
also
S
-local.

(d)

Let
A
be a
p
-torsion group. Consider

m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus p\mathbb{Z}
; we must show that
m.1_{A}
is an isomorphism. For
any
a\in A
we can choose
k\geq 0
such that
p^{k}a=0
, and
p^{k}

is coprime with
m
so we can choose
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
with
p^{k}r+ms=1
.
It follows that
a=msa=(m.1_{A})(sa)
. Using this we see that

m.1_{A}
is surjective. Also, if
ma=0
then
a=sma=0
, so

A[m]=0
, so
m.1_{A}
is also injective.

∎

Proposition 9.17
.

If
A
is a torsion group, then
A_{(0)}=0
and

A_{(p)}=\operatorname{tors}_{p}(A)
.

Proof.

First suppose that
A
is a
p
-torsion group, so

A=\bigcup_{k}A[p^{k}]
. If
m
is not divisible by
p
then we can find

n>0
with
mn=1\pmod{p^{k}}
, so
n.1_{A[p^{k}]}
is inverse to

m.1_{A[p^{k}]}
. It follows that
m.1_{A}
is also an isomorphism.
This holds for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus p\mathbb{Z}
, so
A
is
p
-local, so

A=A_{(p)}
. Now suppose instead that
A
is a
q
-torsion group for
some prime
q\neq p
. For any element
a\in A
we have
q^{v}a=0
for
some
v\geq 0
, so in
A_{(p)}
we have

a/m=(q^{v}a)/(q^{v}m)=0/(q^{v}m)=0
. This shows that
A_{(p)}=0
.
Finally, for a general torsion group
A
we have

A=\bigoplus_{q}\operatorname{tors}_{q}(A)
by Proposition
4.9
, so

A_{(p)}=\bigoplus_{q}\operatorname{tors}_{q}(A)_{(p)}
. By the special cases that we
have just discussed, this sum contains only the single factor

\operatorname{tors}_{p}(A)_{(p)}=\operatorname{tors}_{p}(A)
, as claimed. It is also clear from
Remark
9.4
that
A_{(0)}=0
.
∎

10.
Colimits of sequences

Definition 10.1
.

By a
sequence
we mean a diagram of the form

A_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}A_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}A_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}\dotsb.

Given such a sequence and natural numbers
i\leq j
, we write

f_{ij}
for the composite

A_{i}\xrightarrow{f_{i}}A_{i+1}\xrightarrow{}\dotsb\xrightarrow{f_{j-1}}A_{j}.

In particular,
f_{ii}
is the identity map of
A_{i}
, and

f_{i,i+1}=f_{i}
.

Definition 10.2
.

Given such a sequence, we consider the group
A_{+}=\bigoplus_{i}A_{i}
.
For each
k
we have an inclusion
\iota_{k}\colon A_{k}\to A_{+}
and also a
homomorphism
\iota_{k+1}\circ f_{k}\colon A_{k}\to A_{+}
. We let
R_{k}
denote
the image of
(\iota_{k}-\iota_{k+1}f_{k})\colon A_{k}\to A_{+}
, and put

R_{+}=\sum_{k}R_{k}\leq A_{+}
and
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}=A_{+}/R_{+}
. This group is
called the
colimit
of the sequence.

Next, we write
\overline{\imath}_{k}
for the composite

A_{k}\xrightarrow{\iota_{k}}A_{+}\xrightarrow{}A_{+}/R_{+}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}.

By construction we have
\overline{\imath}_{k}=\overline{\imath}_{k+1}f_{k}
, so the following
diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}

This implies that
\overline{\imath}_{k}=\overline{\imath}_{m}f_{k,m}
whenever
k\leq m
.

Remark 10.3
.

The definition can be reformulated slightly as follows. We can
define an endomorphism
S
of
A_{+}
by

S(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\dotsc)=(0,f_{0}(a_{0}),f_{1}(a_{1}),f_{2}(a_{2}),f_{3}(a_{3}),\dotsb).

Equivalently,
S
is the unique map such that the following diagram
commutes for all
k
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}

We can then say that
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
is the cokernel of

1-S\colon A_{+}\to A_{+}
. Note that if
a_{i}
is the first nonzero entry in

a
then the
i
’th entry in
(1-S)(a)
is again
a_{i}
, so

(1-S)(a)\neq 0
. This shows that
1-S
is injective, so we actually
have a short exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-S}

Remark 10.4
.

The colimit can also be characterised by a universal property, as
follows. A
cone
for the sequence is a group
B
with a
collection of maps
u_{k}\colon A_{k}\to B
such that
u_{k+1}f_{k}=u_{k}
for all

k\geq 0
. By construction, the maps
\overline{\imath}_{k}\colon A_{k}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
form
a cone. We claim that for any cone
\{A_{k}\xrightarrow{u_{k}}B\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

there is a unique homomorphism
u_{\infty}\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}\to B
such that

u_{\infty}\overline{\imath}_{k}=u_{k}
for all
k
. Indeed,
Proposition
3.7
gives us a unique map

v\colon A_{+}\to B
with
vi_{k}=u_{k}
for all
k
, and the cone property
tells us that
v(R_{k})=0
for all
k
, so
v(R_{+})=0
, so
v
induces a
map
u_{\infty}\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}=A_{+}/R_{+}\to B
. This is easily seen to be
the unique map such that
u_{\infty}\overline{\imath}_{k}=u_{k}
for all
k
.

In many cases colimits are just unions, as we now explain.

Proposition 10.5
.

We have

\overline{\imath}_{0}(A_{0})\leq\overline{\imath}_{1}(A_{1})\leq\overline{\imath}_{2}(A_{2})\leq\dotsb\leq\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}.

Moreover,
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
is the union of the groups
\overline{\imath}_{k}(A_{k})
, and
we have
\overline{\imath}_{k}(a)=0
iff
f_{k,m}(a)=0
for some
m\geq k
.

Proof.

First, when
k\leq m
we have
\overline{\imath}_{k}=\overline{\imath}_{m}f_{k,m}
, and this implies
that
\overline{\imath}_{k}(A_{k})\leq\overline{\imath}_{m}(A_{m})
. Next, as
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
is a
quotient of
A_{+}
, we see that every element
a\in\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
can be
written as
a=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\overline{\imath}_{k}(a_{k})
for some
N\geq 0
and

a_{k}\in A_{k}
. Now
\overline{\imath}_{k}(a_{k})\in\overline{\imath}_{k}(A_{k})\leq\overline{\imath}_{N}(A_{N})
for all

k
, so
a\in\overline{\imath}_{N}(A_{N})
. Thus
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}=\bigcup_{N}\overline{\imath}_{N}(A_{N})
as
claimed. Now suppose we have
a\in A_{k}
and that
f_{km}(a)=0
for
some
m\geq k
. Using
\overline{\imath}_{k}=\overline{\imath}_{m}f_{km}
we deduce that

\overline{\imath}_{k}(a)=0
. Conversely, suppose that
\overline{\imath}_{k}(a)=0
, so

i_{k}(a)\in R_{+}
, so

i_{k}(a)=\sum_{m=0}^{N-1}(i_{m}(b_{m})-i_{m+1}(f_{m}(b_{m})))

for some
N>k
and some
b_{0},\dotsc,b_{N-1}
with
b_{i}\in A_{i}
. Now
let
h\colon\bigoplus_{m=0}^{N}A_{m}\to A_{N}
be the map given by
f_{mN}
on

A_{m}
, or more formally the unique map with
hi_{m}=f_{mN}
. We note
that

h(i_{m}(b_{m})-i_{m+1}(f_{m}(b_{m})))=f_{m,N}(b_{m})-f_{m+1,N}(f_{m}(b_{m}))=0,

so we can apply
h
to the above equation for
i_{k}(a)
to get

f_{kN}(a)=0
as required.
∎

Corollary 10.6
.

Suppose we have a sequence
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and a cone

\{u_{i}\colon A_{i}\to B\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
giving rise to a homomorphism

u_{\infty}\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}\to B
. Then

(a)

The image of
u_{\infty}
is the union of the subgroups

u_{k}(A_{k})
.

(b)

The map
u_{\infty}
is injective iff whenever
u_{k}(a)=0
,
there exists
m\geq k
with
f_{km}(a)=0
.

Proof.

This is clear from the Proposition.
∎

Example 10.7
.

Let
A
be any abelian group. Suppose we have a chain of subgroups

A_{0}\leq A_{1}\leq A_{2}\leq A_{3}\leq\dotsb\leq A.

Let
f_{k}\colon A_{k}\to A_{k+1}
be the inclusion. Then the colimit of the
resulting sequence is just
\bigcup_{i}A_{i}
. Indeed, the inclusions

A_{n}\to\bigcup_{i}A_{i}
form a cone, which clearly has both the
properties in Corollary
10.6
.

The following examples are instructive as well as useful.

Proposition 10.8
.

Let
A
be an arbitrary abelian group. Then the colimit of the
sequence

A\xrightarrow{n}A\xrightarrow{n}A\xrightarrow{n}A\xrightarrow{n}A\xrightarrow{}\dotsb

is
A[1/n]
, whereas the colimit of the sequence

A\xrightarrow{1}A\xrightarrow{2}A\xrightarrow{3}A\xrightarrow{4}A\xrightarrow{}\dotsb

is the rationalisation
A_{(0)}
.

Proof.

We will prove the second statement; the first is similar but easier.
Let
C
denote the colimit, so we have maps
\overline{\imath}_{k}\colon A\to C
with

\overline{\imath}_{k}(a)=(k+1)\overline{\imath}_{k+1}(a)
. Define
u_{n}\colon A\to A_{(0)}
by

u_{n}(a)=a/n!
. As
((n+1)a)/(n+1)!=a/n!
we see that these maps form
a cone, so there is a unique map
u_{\infty}\colon C\to A_{(0)}
with

u_{\infty}\overline{\imath}_{k}=u_{k}
for all
k
. Any element
a\in A_{(0)}
can be
written as
a=a^{\prime}/n
for some
a^{\prime}\in A
and
n>0
, so

a=((n-1)!a^{\prime})/n!=u_{n}((n-1)!a^{\prime})
, so
A_{(0)}
is the union of the
images of the maps
u_{n}
. Now suppose that
u_{n}(a^{\prime})=0
. By the
definition of
A_{(0)}
this just means that
ma^{\prime}=0
for some
m>0
.
The map
f_{n,n+m}
in our sequence is multiplication by the integer

p=(n+1)(n+2)\dotsb(n+m)=\frac{(n+m)!}{n!}=m!\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{n+m}\\
{n}\end{array}\right),

which is divisible by
m
, so
f_{n,n+m}(a^{\prime})=0
. The claim follows
by Corollary
10.6
.
∎

Proposition 10.9
.

Suppose we have a commutative diagram as shown
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}

Let
\overline{\imath}_{k}
be the canonical map
A_{k}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
, and let
\overline{\jmath}_{k}

be the canonical map
B_{k}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}
. Then there is a unique map

p_{\infty}
such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\jmath}_{k}}

commutes for all
k
. Moreover, if all the maps
p_{k}
are injective,
or surjective, or bijective, then
p_{\infty}
has the same property.

Proof.

The maps
\overline{\jmath}_{k}p_{k}\colon A_{k}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}
satisfy

\overline{\jmath}_{k}p_{k}=\overline{\jmath}_{k+1}g_{k}p_{k}=\overline{\jmath}_{k+1}p_{k+1}f_{k},

so they form a cone for the sequence
\{A_{i}\}
. There is thus a
unique map
p_{\infty}\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}
with

p_{\infty}\overline{\imath}_{k}=\overline{\jmath}_{k}p_{k}
for all
k
, as claimed.

(a)

Now suppose that all the maps
p_{k}
are injective.
Consider an element
a\in\ker(p_{\infty})
. By
Proposition
10.5
we have
a=\overline{\imath}_{k}(a^{\prime})
for
some
k
and some
a^{\prime}\in A_{k}
. We then have

\overline{\jmath}_{k}(p_{k}(a^{\prime}))=p_{\infty}(\overline{\imath}_{k}(a^{\prime}))=p_{\infty}(a)=0
. The same
proposition therefore tells us that
g_{km}(p_{k}(a^{\prime}))=0
for some

m\geq k
. Now
g_{km}p_{k}=p_{m}f_{km}
and
p_{m}
is injective, so

f_{km}(a^{\prime})=0
. This means that
a=i_{k}(a^{\prime})=i_{m}(f_{km}(a^{\prime}))=0
.
Thus,
p_{\infty}
is injective as claimed.

(b)

Suppose instead that all the maps
p_{k}
are surjective.
Consider an element
b\in\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}
. By
Proposition
10.5
we have
b=\overline{\jmath}_{k}(b^{\prime})
for
some
k
and some
b^{\prime}\in B_{k}
. As
p_{k}
is surjective, we can
choose
a^{\prime}\in A_{k}
with
p_{k}(a^{\prime})=b^{\prime}
, and then put
b=\overline{\imath}_{k}(a)
;
we find that
p_{\infty}(a)=b
. Thus,
p_{\infty}
is also
surjective.

(c)

If the maps
p_{k}
are all isomorphisms, then (a) and (b)
together imply that
p_{\infty}
is an isomorphism.

∎

Proposition 10.10
.

Suppose we have a commutative diagram as shown, in which all the
columns are exact:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}

Then the resulting sequence

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}\xrightarrow{p_{\infty}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}\xrightarrow{q_{\infty}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}C_{i}

is also exact.

Proof.

First, any element
a\in\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
has the form
a=\overline{\imath}_{n}(a^{\prime})
for
some
n
and
a^{\prime}
. We can then chase
a^{\prime}
around the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\jmath}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{k}_{n}}

to see that
q_{\infty}(p_{\infty}(a))=0
. Conversely, suppose we have
an element
b\in\ker(q_{\infty})
. We then have
b=\overline{\jmath}_{n}(b^{\prime})
for some

n
and some
b^{\prime}\in B_{n}
. We then have

\overline{k}_{n}(q_{n}(b^{\prime}))=q_{\infty}(\overline{\jmath}_{n}(b^{\prime}))=q_{\infty}(b)=0
, so

h_{n,m}(q_{n}(b^{\prime}))=0
for some
m\geq n
. Now

h_{n,m}(q_{n}(b^{\prime}))=q_{m}(g_{n,m}(b^{\prime}))
, so

g_{n,m}(b^{\prime})\in\ker(q_{m})=\operatorname{image}(p_{m})
, so we can find
a^{\prime}\in A_{m}

with
p_{m}(a^{\prime})=g_{n,m}(b^{\prime})
. Now put
a=\overline{\imath}_{m}(a^{\prime})
. We find that

p_{\infty}(a)=\overline{\jmath}_{m}(p_{m}(a^{\prime}))=\overline{\jmath}_{m}(g_{n,m}(b^{\prime}))=\overline{\jmath}_{n}(b^{\prime})=b,

so
b\in\operatorname{image}(p_{\infty})
. The claim follows.
∎

Proposition 10.11
.

Suppose we have a sequence

A_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}A_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}A_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}\dotsb

and a nondecreasing function
u\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
u(i)\to\infty

as
i\to\infty
. Put

g_{i}=f_{u(i),u(i+1)}=(A_{u(i)}\xrightarrow{f_{u(i)}}A_{u(i)+1}\xrightarrow{f_{u(i)+1}}\dotsb\xrightarrow{f_{u(i+1)-1}}A_{u(i+1)}),

so we have a sequence

A_{u(0)}\xrightarrow{g_{0}}A_{u(1)}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}A_{u(2)}\xrightarrow{g_{2}}A_{u(3)}\xrightarrow{g_{3}}\dotsb

Then there is a canonical isomorphism
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{\imath}_{n}\colon A_{n}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
and

\overline{\jmath}_{n}\colon A_{u(n)}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}
be the usual maps. As

\overline{\imath}_{n}=\overline{\imath}_{n+1}f_{n}
for all
n
we find by induction that

\overline{\imath}_{n}=\overline{\imath}_{m}f_{n,m}
for all
n\leq m
. By applying this to the pair

u(k)\leq u(k+1)
, we see that
\overline{\imath}_{u(k)}=\overline{\imath}_{u(k+1)}g_{k}
, so the
maps
\overline{\imath}_{u(k)}
form a cone for the sequence

\{A_{u(j)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, so there is a unique map

p\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
with
p\overline{\jmath}_{k}=\overline{\imath}_{u(k)}
for all

k
. In the opposite direction, suppose we have
n\in\mathbb{N}
. As

u(j)\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
, we can choose
k
such that

n\leq u(k)
, and form the composite

q_{nk}=(A_{n}\xrightarrow{f_{n,u(k)}}A_{u(k)}\xrightarrow{\overline{\jmath}_{k}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}).

As
\overline{\jmath}_{k}=\overline{\jmath}_{k+1}g_{k}=\overline{\jmath}_{k+1}f_{u(k),u(k+1)}
and

f_{u(k),u(k+1)}f_{n,u(k)}=f_{n,u(k+1)}
we see that

q_{n,k}=q_{n,k+1}
. Thus
q_{nk}
is independent of
k
, so we can
denote it by
q_{n}
. We also find that
q_{n}=q_{n+1}f_{n}
, so the maps

q_{n}
form a cone for the sequence
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, so there is a
unique map
q\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}
with
q\overline{\imath}_{n}=q_{n}
for
all
n
.

Now note that any element
a\in\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
has the form
a=\overline{\imath}_{n}(a^{\prime})

for some
n
and
a^{\prime}\in A_{n}
. If we choose
k
with
u(k)\geq n
we
have

pq(a)=pq_{n}(a^{\prime})=p\overline{\jmath}_{k}f_{n,u(k)}(a^{\prime})=\overline{\imath}_{u(k)}f_{n,u(k)}(a^{\prime})=\overline{\imath}_{n}(a^{\prime})=a,

so
pq
is the identity. A similar argument shows that
qp
is the
identity.
∎

Proposition 10.12
.

Suppose we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{02}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{03}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{11}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{12}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{13}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{20}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{21}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{22}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{23}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{30}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{31}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{32}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{33}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{00}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{00}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{02}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{02}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{03}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{03}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{10}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{10}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{11}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{11}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{13}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{13}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{20}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{20}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{22}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{22}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{30}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{30}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{31}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{31}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{32}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{32}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{33}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{33}}

and thus sequences

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{0j}\xrightarrow{g_{0\infty}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{1j}\xrightarrow{g_{1\infty}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{2j}\xrightarrow{g_{2\infty}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{3j}\xrightarrow{g_{3\infty}}\dotsb

and

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i0}\xrightarrow{f_{\infty 0}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i1}\xrightarrow{f_{\infty 1}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i2}\xrightarrow{f_{\infty 2}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i3}\xrightarrow{f_{\infty 3}}\dotsb

Suppose we also put

h_{i}=g_{i,j+1}f_{ij}=f_{i+1,j}g_{ij}\colon A_{ii}\to A_{i+1,i+1},

giving a third sequence

A_{00}\xrightarrow{h_{0}}A_{11}\xrightarrow{h_{1}}A_{22}\xrightarrow{h_{2}}A_{33}\xrightarrow{h_{3}}\dotsb

Then there are canonical isomorphisms

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{ij}\simeq\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ii}\simeq\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ij}.

Example 10.13
.

In conjunction with Proposition
10.8
, this
will give

A[\frac{1}{n}][\frac{1}{m}]=A[\frac{1}{nm}]=A[\frac{1}{m}][\frac{1}{n}]

Proof.

Put
A_{++}=\bigoplus_{n,m}A_{nm}
, and let

i_{nm}\colon A_{nm}\to A_{++}
be the canonical inclusion. Put

\displaystyle P_{nm}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{image}(i_{nm}-i_{n,m+1}f_{nm}\colon A_{nm}\to A_{++})

\displaystyle Q_{nm}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{image}(i_{nm}-i_{n+1,m}g_{nm}\colon A_{nm}\to A_{++}).

From the definitions we have

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{nj}

\displaystyle=A_{++}/\sum_{n,m}P_{nm}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{m}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{im}

\displaystyle=A_{++}/\sum_{n,m}Q_{nm}.

It follows easily that

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{ij}=A_{++}/\left(\sum_{n,m}P_{nm}+\sum_{n,m}Q_{nm}\right)=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{j}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ij}.

We write
A_{\infty\infty}
for this group, and we write
\overline{\imath}_{nm}

for the obvious map
A_{nm}\to A_{\infty\infty}
. By construction,
the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{nm}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n,m+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\infty\infty}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{nm}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{nm}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{nm}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{n,m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\imath}_{n+1,m}}

It follows that
\overline{\imath}_{kk}=\overline{\imath}_{k+1,k+1}h_{k}
, so the maps
\overline{\imath}_{kk}

form a cone for the sequence
\{A_{ii}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Thus, if we
write
\overline{\jmath}_{k}
for the usual map
A_{kk}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ii}
, we find
that there is a unique map
p\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ii}\to A_{\infty\infty}

with
p\overline{\jmath}_{k}=\overline{\imath}_{kk}
for all
k
. In the opposite direction,
suppose we have
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
n,m\leq k
. By composing
f
’s
and
g
’s in various orders we can form a number of maps

A_{nm}\to A_{kk}
but they are all the same because the original
diagram is commutative. We write
u_{nmk}
for this map, and put

q_{nmk}=\overline{\jmath}_{k}u_{nmk}\colon A_{nm}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ii}
. Now

\overline{\jmath}_{k}=\overline{\jmath}_{k+1}h_{k}
and
h_{k}u_{nmk}=u_{n,m,k+1}
so

q_{n,m,k}=q_{n,m,k+1}
. Thus
q_{nmk}
is independent of
k

(provided that
k\geq\max(n,m)
) so we can denote it by
q_{nm}
. We
can now put the maps
q_{nm}
together to give a map

q^{\prime}\colon A_{++}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ii}
with
q^{\prime}i_{nm}=q_{nm}
. When

k>\max(n,m)
we have
u_{nmk}=u_{n,m+1,k}f_{nm}=u_{n+1,m,k}g_{nm}
,
and using this we see that
q^{\prime}(P_{nm})=0=q^{\prime}(Q_{nm})
. There is thus
an induced map
A_{\infty\infty}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{ii}
with

q\overline{\imath}_{nm}=q_{nm}
. We leave it to the reader to check that
q
is
inverse to
p
.
∎

11.
Limits and derived limits of towers

Definition 11.1
.

A
tower
is a diagram of the form

B_{0}\xleftarrow{f_{0}}B_{1}\xleftarrow{f_{1}}B_{2}\xleftarrow{f_{2}}B_{3}\xleftarrow{f_{3}}\dotsb

Given such a tower an integers
i\geq j
, we write
f_{ij}
for the
composite

B_{i}\xrightarrow{f_{i-1}}B_{i-1}\xrightarrow{f_{i-2}}\dotsb\xrightarrow{f_{j}}B_{j}.

Note that
f_{ii}
is the identity map, and
f_{i+1,i}=f_{i}
, and

f_{jk}f_{ij}=f_{ik}
whenever
i\geq j\geq k
.

Definition 11.2
.

Suppose we have a tower as above. The
limit
(or
inverse
limit
) of the tower is the group

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}=\{a\in\prod_{i}B_{i}\;|\;a_{i}=f_{i}(a_{i+1})\text{ for all }i\}.

Equivalently, if we define
D\colon\prod_{i}B_{i}\to\prod_{i}B_{i}
by

D(a)_{i}=a_{i}-f_{i}(a_{i+1})
, then
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}=\ker(D)
. We also
write
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}{}_{i}^{1}B_{i}
for the cokernel of
D
. We define

p_{n}\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}\to B_{n}
by
p_{n}(a)=a_{n}
, so
p_{n}=f_{n}p_{n+1}
.

Remark 11.3
.

The limit can also be characterised by a universal property, as
follows. A
cone
for the tower is a group
A
with a
collection of maps
u_{k}\colon A\to B_{k}
such that
u_{k}=f_{k}u_{k+1}
for all

k
. Tautologically, the maps
p_{k}\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}\to B_{k}
form a
cone. Moreover, for any cone
\{A\xrightarrow{u_{k}}B_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
we can
define
u_{\infty}\colon A\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}
by

u_{\infty}(a)=(u_{0}(a),u_{1}(a),u_{2}(a),\dotsc),

and this is the unique map with
p_{k}u_{\infty}=u_{k}
for all
k
.

Example 11.4
.

Suppose we have a chain of subgroups

B_{0}\geq B_{1}\geq B_{2}\geq\dotsb

and we take the maps
f_{i}
to be the inclusion maps. Then we see
from the definitions that

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}=\{(b,b,b,\dotsb)\;|\;b\in\bigcap_{i}B_{i}\}\simeq\bigcap_{i}B_{i}.

Example 11.5
.

Suppose we have a system of groups
C_{i}
(for
i\in\mathbb{N}
). Put

B_{k}=\prod_{i=0}^{k}C_{i}
and let
f_{k}\colon B_{k+1}\to B_{k}
be the
obvious projection map. If
b\in\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}B_{k}
then

b_{k}\in\prod_{i=0}^{k}C_{i}
so
b_{kk}\in C_{k}
. We can thus define

d\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}B_{k}\to\prod_{k}C_{k}

by

d(b)=(b_{00},b_{11},b_{22},\dotsc).

By the definition of
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}B_{k}
, we have
b_{kj}=b_{jj}
for

j\leq k
. Using this, we see that
d
is an isomorphism.

Example 11.6
.

Suppose we have a tower in which the groups are arbitrary but the
maps are all zero. Then the map
D
is the identity, so both

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}
and
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}
are zero.

Example 11.7
.

Suppose we have a sequence

A_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}A_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}A_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}\dotsb,

and another group
B
. This gives us a tower

\operatorname{Hom}(A_{0},B)\xleftarrow{f_{0}^{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(A_{1},B)\xleftarrow{f_{1}^{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(A_{2},B)\xleftarrow{f_{2}^{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(A_{3},B)\xleftarrow{f_{3}^{*}}\dotsb

The elements of
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}\operatorname{Hom}(A_{i},B)
are precisely the cones from
the sequence
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
to
B
, which biject with
homomorphisms from
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
to
B
. In other words, we have

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}\operatorname{Hom}(A_{i},B)=\operatorname{Hom}(\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i},B).

Example 11.8
.

Fix a prime
p
. We then have a tower

\mathbb{Z}/p\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{Z}/p^{3}\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{Z}/p^{4}\xleftarrow{}\dotsb.

The inverse limit is called
the ring of
p
-adic integers
, and
is denoted by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. We will investigate it in more detail in
Section
12
. We can also form a tower

\mathbb{Z}/0!\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{Z}/1!\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{Z}/2!\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{Z}/3!\xleftarrow{}\dotsb

The inverse limit is called
the profinite completion of
\mathbb{Z}
,
and is denoted by
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
. Using the Chinese Remainder
Theorem (Proposition
4.7
) one can show that

\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. For yet another description, recall that

{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}}=\bigcup_{n}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})[n!]=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longrightarrow}{lim}}_{n}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})[n!],

so

\operatorname{End}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{n}\operatorname{Hom}(({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})[n!],{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}}).

Now we have a map
m\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{End}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})
defined by
m(k)=k.1_{{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}}}
,
and this fits in a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{End}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/n!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}(({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})[n!],{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
restrict
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{n}}

Using the fact that
({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})[n!]
is generated by
(1/n!)+\mathbb{Z}
we see
that
m_{n}
is an isomorphism. By passing to inverse limits, we
obtain an isomorphism
m_{\infty}\colon\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\to\operatorname{End}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})
.

Proposition 11.9
.

Suppose we have a commutative diagram as shown
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}

and we define
p=\prod_{i}p_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to\prod_{i}B_{i}
. Then the
central square below commutes, so there are induced maps
p_{\infty}

and
p^{1}_{\infty}
as shown.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}{}_{i}^{1}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}{}^{1}B_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{1}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

Proof.

Clear from the definitions.
∎

Proposition 11.10
.

Suppose we have a commutative diagram as shown, in which all the
columns are short exact:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}

Then there is an associated exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}C_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{1}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{1}_{\infty}}

Proof.

Apply the Snake Lemma to the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}C_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}q_{i}}

in which the rows are easily seen to be short exact.
∎

In practice the groups
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}
are usually either zero, or
enormous and untractable. We will thus be very interested in results
that force them to be zero.

Proposition 11.11
.

Suppose we have a tower in which the maps
f_{i}\colon A_{i+1}\to A_{i}
are
all surjective. Then
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}=0
, and the projection maps

p_{k}\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}\to A_{k}
are all surjective.

Proof.

Consider an element
a\in\prod_{i}A_{i}
. We will choose elements

b_{k}\in A_{k}
recursively as follows: we start with
b_{0}=0
, and then
take
b_{n}
to be any element with
f_{n-1}(b_{n})=b_{n-1}-a_{n-1}
.
These elements
b_{k}
give an element
b\in\prod_{i}A_{i}
with
D(b)=a
,
so
D
is surjective and
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}=\operatorname{cok}(D)=0
.

Now suppose we have an element
a\in A_{k}
. Define
c_{i}=f_{k,i}(a)

for all
i\leq k
. Then define
c_{i}\in A_{i}
recursively for
i>k
by
choosing
c_{i}
to be any element with
f_{i-1}(c_{i})=c_{i-1}
. This
gives an element
c\in\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
with
p_{k}(c)=a
.
∎

Definition 11.12
.

We say that a tower
A_{0}\xleftarrow{f_{0}}A_{1}\xleftarrow{f_{1}}\dotsb
is

nilpotent
if for all
i
there exists
j>i
such that

f_{ji}=0\colon A_{j}\to A_{i}
.

Proposition 11.13
.

For a nilpotent tower as above, we have

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}^{1}A_{i}=0
.

Proof.

Define
E\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to\prod_{i}A_{i}
by

E(a)_{i}=\sum_{j=i}^{\infty}f_{j,i}(a_{j}).

Although the sum is formally infinite, the nilpotence hypothesis
means that there are only finitely many nonzero terms, so the
expression is meaningful. It is then not hard to check that

DE=ED=1
, so the kernel and cokernel of
D
are zero.
∎

Definition 11.14
.

Consider a tower
A_{0}\xleftarrow{f_{0}}A_{1}\xleftarrow{f_{1}}\dotsb
, so for each
i
we
have a descending chain of subgroups

A_{i}\geq f_{i+1,i}(A_{i+1})\geq f_{i+2,i}(A_{i+2})\geq f_{i+3,i}(A_{i+3})\geq\dotsb

We say that the tower is
Mittag-Leffler
if for each
i
there
exists
j\geq i
such that
f_{ki}(A_{k})=f_{ji}(A_{j})
for all

k\geq j
(so the above chain is eventually constant).

Example 11.15
.

Towers of surjections are Mittag-Leffler, as are nilpotent towers.

Proposition 11.16
.

If all the groups
A_{i}
are finite, then the tower is
Mittag-Leffler. Similarly, if the groups
A_{i}
are
finite-dimensional vector spaces over a field
K
, and the maps
f_{i}

are all
K
-linear, then the tower is Mittag-Leffler.

Proof.

In the first case, we just choose
j\geq i
such that the order

|f_{ji}(A_{j})|
is as small as possible; it then follows that

f_{ki}(A_{k})=f_{ji}(A_{j})
for
k\geq i
. In the second case, use
dimensions instead of orders.
∎

Proposition 11.17
.

If
A
is a Mittag-Leffler tower, we have
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}^{1}A_{i}=0
.

Proof.

By the Mittag-Leffler condition, there is a subgroup
A^{\prime}_{i}\leq A_{i}

such that
f_{ji}(A_{j})=A^{\prime}_{i}
for all sufficiently large
j
. Thus,
for
j
very large we have both
A^{\prime}_{i+1}=f_{j,i+1}(A_{j})
and

A^{\prime}_{i}=f_{ji}(A_{j})=f_{i}(f_{j,i+1}(A_{j}))=f_{i}(A^{\prime}_{i+1})
. Thus, the
groups
A^{\prime}_{i}
form a subtower of
A
, with surjective maps

f^{\prime}_{i}\colon A^{\prime}_{i+1}\to A^{\prime}_{i}
. Now put
A^{\prime\prime}_{i}=A_{i}/A^{\prime}_{i}
, so there are
induced maps
f^{\prime\prime}_{i}\colon A^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}\to A^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, giving a third tower. If

j
is much larger than
i
we have
f_{ji}(A_{j})=A^{\prime}_{i}
and so

f^{\prime\prime}_{ji}=0\colon A^{\prime\prime}_{j}\to A^{\prime\prime}_{i}
; this shows that the tower
A^{\prime\prime}
is
nilpotent. Now apply Proposition
11.10
to the
short exact sequence
A^{\prime}\to A\to A^{\prime\prime}
to give an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A^{\prime}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A^{\prime\prime}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A^{\prime}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A^{\prime\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Here
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}^{1}A^{\prime}_{i}=0
because the maps in
A^{\prime}
are surjective, and

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}^{1}A^{\prime\prime}_{i}=0
because
A^{\prime\prime}
is nilpotent, so

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}^{1}A_{i}=0
as claimed. (We also have
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A^{\prime\prime}_{i}=0
and
so
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A^{\prime}_{i}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
.)
∎

We also have the following result analogous to
Proposition
10.11
, which again indicates that

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
and
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}
only depend on the asymptotic
behaviour of the tower.

Proposition 11.18
.

Suppose we have a tower

A_{0}\xleftarrow{f_{0}}A_{1}\xleftarrow{f_{1}}A_{2}\xleftarrow{f_{2}}A_{3}\xleftarrow{f_{3}}\dotsb

and a nondecreasing function
u\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
u(i)\to\infty

as
i\to\infty
. Put

g_{i}=f_{u(i+1),u(i)}=(A_{u(i+1)}\xrightarrow{f_{u(i+1)-1}}A_{u(i+1)-1}\dotsb\xrightarrow{f_{u(i)}}A_{u(i)}),

so we have a tower

A_{u(0)}\xleftarrow{g_{0}}A_{u(1)}\xleftarrow{g_{1}}A_{u(2)}\xleftarrow{g_{2}}A_{u(3)}\xleftarrow{g_{3}}\dotsb

Then there are canonical isomorphisms

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}
and

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{j}A_{u(j)}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}
.

Proof.

Define
v\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
v(i)=\min\{j\;|\;u(j)\geq i\}
. We will
construct a diagram as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{j}A_{u(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{j}A_{u(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{j}A_{u(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{j}A_{u(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

The maps are:

\displaystyle D^{\prime}(b)_{j}

\displaystyle=b_{j}-f_{u(j+1),u(j)}(b_{j+1})

\displaystyle D(a)_{i}

\displaystyle=a_{i}-f_{i+1,i}(a_{i+1})

\displaystyle\phi(b)_{i}

\displaystyle=f_{uv(i),i}(b_{v(i)})

\displaystyle\psi(a)_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{u(j)}

\displaystyle\lambda(b)_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u(j)=i}b_{j}

\displaystyle\mu(a)_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u(j)\leq i<u(j+1)}f_{i,u(j)}(a_{i}).

Thus
D
and
D^{\prime}
are the usual maps whose kernels and cokernels are
the
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}
and
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}
groups under consideration. We claim
that the diagram commutes. To see this, consider a point

b\in\prod_{j}A_{u(j)}
. We then have

\displaystyle D(\phi(b))_{i}

\displaystyle=\phi(b)_{i}-f_{i+1,i}(\phi(b)_{i+1})

\displaystyle=f_{uv(i),i}(b_{v(i)})-f_{uv(i+1),i}(b_{v(i+1)})

\displaystyle\lambda(D^{\prime}(b))_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u(j)=i}D^{\prime}(b)_{j}=\sum_{u(j)=i}(b_{j}-f_{u(j+1),u(j)}b_{j+1}).

If
u^{-1}\{i\}=\emptyset
we find that
v(i+1)=v(i)
and so

D(\phi(b))_{i}=0=\lambda(D^{\prime}(b))_{i}
. If
u^{-1}\{i\}
is nonempty then it
will be an interval, say
u^{-1}\{i\}=\{j_{0},\dotsc,j_{1}-1\}
. In our
expression for
\lambda(D^{\prime}(b))_{i}
, the map
f_{u(j+1),u(j)}
is just the
identity except when
j=j_{1}-1
. The expression therefore cancels
down to
b_{j_{0}}-f_{u(j_{1}),i}(b_{j_{1}})
. On the
other hand, we also find that
v(i)=j_{0}
and
v(i+1)=j_{1}
, so

D(\phi(b))_{i}=f_{ii}(b_{j_{0}})-f_{u(j_{1}),i}(b_{j_{1}})=\lambda(D^{\prime}(b))_{i}.

Thus, the left square commutes. For the right square, consider an
element
a\in\prod_{i}A_{i}
. We have

\displaystyle\mu(D(a))_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u(j)\leq i<u(j+1)}f_{i,u(j)}(D(a)_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{u(j)\leq i<u(j+1)}(f_{i,u(j)}(a_{i})-f_{i+1,u(j)}(a_{i+1}))

\displaystyle=a_{u(j)}-f_{u(j+1),u(j)}(a_{u(j+1)})

\displaystyle=\psi(a)_{j}-f_{u(j+1),u(j)}(\psi(a)_{j+1})=D^{\prime}(\psi(a))_{j}

as required. We therefore have induced maps

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}\xrightarrow{\phi^{\prime}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A_{i}\xrightarrow{\psi^{\prime}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{j}A_{u(j)}

and

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{j}A_{u(j)}\xrightarrow{\lambda^{\prime}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A_{i}\xrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{j}A_{u(j)}.

It is straightforward to check that
\phi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}
are
the respective identity maps. Now define

\sigma\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to\prod_{i}A_{i}
by

\sigma(a)_{i}=\sum_{i\leq h<uv(i)}f_{h,i}(a_{h}).

We claim that
\lambda\mu+D\sigma=1
(which implies that
\lambda^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}=1
).
The proof that
\lambda(\mu(a))_{i}+D(\sigma(a))_{i}=a_{i}
splits into two cases,
depending on whether
i\in\operatorname{image}(u)
or not. If
i\not\in\operatorname{image}(u)

we find that the inequality
i\leq u(v(i))
cannot be an equality, so

v(i+1)=v(i)
. Using this we deduce that
D(\sigma(a))_{i}
is the
difference between two sums that mostly have the same terms, and thus
that
D(\sigma(a))_{i}=a_{i}
. On the other hand, we have

\lambda(\mu(a))_{i}=\sum_{u(j)=i}\mu(a)_{j}
which is zero as the sum has no
terms. Now consider instead the case where
i\in\operatorname{image}(u)
. Let
j_{1}

be the largest integer such that
u(j_{1})=i
, and put
i^{\prime}=u(j_{1}+1)>i
.
From the definitions we have

\lambda(\mu(a))_{i}=\sum_{u(j)=i}\sum_{u(j)\leq h<u(j+1)}f_{h,u(j)}(a_{h}).

However, the inner summation is empty unless
j=j_{1}
, so the formula
reduces to
\lambda(\mu(a))_{i}=\sum_{i\leq h<i^{\prime}}f_{h,i}(a_{h})
. We also
have
uv(i)=i
, so
\sigma(a)_{i}=0
, and
uv(i+1)=u(j_{1}+1)=i^{\prime}
, so

\sigma(a)_{i+1}=\sum_{i<h<i^{\prime}}h_{h,i+1}(a_{h})
. From this it is easy to
see that
\lambda(\mu(a))_{i}+D(\sigma(a))_{i}=a_{i}
as required.

We now consider instead the map
\mu\lambda
. Define

\tau\colon\prod_{j}A_{u(j)}\to\prod_{j}A_{u(j)}
by

\tau(b)_{j}=\sum_{k<j,\;u(k)=u(j)}b_{k}.

We claim that
\mu\lambda=1+D\tau
(which implies that
\mu^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime}=1
).
The proof that
\mu(\lambda(b))_{j}=b_{j}+D(\tau(b))_{j}
again splits into two
cases. First suppose that
u(j+1)=u(j)
. It is then immediate from
the definitions that
\mu(\lambda(b))_{j}=0
. On the other hand, the sums
defining
\tau(b)_{j}
and
\tau(b)_{j+1}
differ only by a single
term, so
D(\tau(b))_{j}=-b_{j}
, as required. Now suppose instead
that
u(j+1)>u(j)
. In this case we have
\tau(b)_{j+1}=0
, so

b_{j}+D(\tau(b))_{j}=b_{j}+\tau(b)_{j}=b_{j}+\sum_{k<j,\;u(k)=u(j)}b_{k}=\sum_{u(k)=u(j)}b_{k}.

On the other hand, we have

\mu(\lambda(b))_{j}=\sum_{u(j)\leq i<u(j+1)}\sum_{u(k)=i}f_{i,u(j)}(b_{k}).

The inner sum has no terms unless
i=u(j)
, and in that context

f_{i,u(j)}
is the identity, so the above reduces to

\mu(\lambda(b))_{j}=\sum_{u(k)=u(j)}b_{k}=b_{j}+D(\tau(b))_{j}

as required.
∎

12.
Completion and derived completion

Definition 12.1
.

Let
p
be a prime. For any abelian group
A
we have a tower of
surjections

0=A/p^{0}\xleftarrow{}A/p\xleftarrow{}A/p^{2}\xleftarrow{}A/p^{3}\xleftarrow{}\dotsb

We write
A_{p}
for the inverse limit of this tower, and call this the

p
-completion
of
A
. In particular, we have a group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
whose elements are called
p
-adic integers
. For any
A
we
have a homomorphism
\eta\colon A\to A_{p}
given by

\eta(a)=(a+p^{0}A,a+p^{1}A,a+p^{2}A,a+p^{3}A,\dotsc).

We say that
A
is
p
-complete
if
\eta
is an isomorphism.

Example 12.2
.

Let
A
be a finite abelian group, so
A
splits as
B\oplus C
say,
where
|B|
is a power of
p
and
|C|
is coprime to
p
. For all

k
we have
p^{k}C=C
, and for large
k
we have
p^{k}B=0
. It follows
that
A_{p}=B
. In particular, we see from this that finite abelian

p
-groups are
p
-complete.

Example 12.3
.

Suppose that
A
is divisible. Then for all
k
we have
p^{k}A=A
, so

A/p^{k}=0
; it follows that
A_{p}=0
. In particular, we have
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}=0

and
({\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Z})_{p}=0
.

Remark 12.4
.

The symbol
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is often used for
{\mathbb{Q}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, which is not zero;
it is known as the field of
p
-adic rationals. However, this is
different from the group that we have called
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, which is
trivial.

Proposition 12.5
.

For all
A
and
k\geq 0
the projection
\pi_{k}\colon A\to A/p^{k}A
induces
an isomorphism
A_{p}/p^{k}A_{p}=A/p^{k}A
.

Proof.

For notational simplicity, we will treat only the case
k=1
. The
general case is essentially the same, after we have used
Proposition
11.18
to identify
A_{p}
with the
inverse limit of the sequence

A/p^{k}\xleftarrow{}A/p^{2k}\xleftarrow{}A/p^{3k}\xleftarrow{}\dotsb.

First, the projection
\pi_{1}\colon\prod_{i}A/p^{i}\to A/p
restricts to give
a homomorphism
\phi\colon A_{p}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A/p^{i}\to A/p
.
Proposition
11.11
tells us that this is
surjective. As
A/p
has exponent
p
, the subgroup
pA_{p}
is
contained in the kernel. We need to prove that the kernel is
precisely
pA_{p}
. Suppose that
a\in\ker(\phi)
. Choose
a_{i}\in A

representing the component of
a
in
A/p^{i}A
. As
A/p^{0}A=0
and

\phi(a)=0
we can take
a_{0}=a_{1}=0
. By the definition of the
inverse limit we see that
a_{i}
is the image of
a_{i+1}
in

A/p^{i}A
, so
a_{i+1}=a_{i}+p^{i}b_{i}
for some
b_{i}\in A
(and we may
take
b_{0}=0
). Now put
c_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p^{i-1}b_{i}\in A
. It is
visible that
c_{n+1}=c_{n}+p^{n}b_{n+1}=c_{n}\pmod{p^{n}A_{n}}
, so the cosets

c_{n}+p^{n}A
define an element
c\in A_{p}
. We also see by induction
that
pc_{k}=a_{k+1}=a_{k}\pmod{p^{k}A_{k}}
, so
pc=a
. Thus
a\in pA_{p}
as
claimed.
∎

Remark 12.6
.

We see from the proposition that
A_{p}=(A_{p})_{p}
, so
A_{p}
is

p
-complete, as one would expect. There is a subtlety analogous to
Remark
9.15
here; we leave it to the reader to
check that the two natural maps
A_{p}\to(A_{p})_{p}
are the same, and
they are both isomorphisms.

We next examine the structure of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
in more detail. First, as the
groups
\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
have canonical ring structures, and the maps

\mathbb{Z}/p^{k+1}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
are ring maps, we see that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a subring
of
\prod_{k}\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
. We will write
\pi_{k}
for the projection

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
, which is a surjective ring homomorphism. Note also
that for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
\eta(n)=0
iff
\pi_{k}\eta(n)=0
for all
k

iff
n
is divisible by
p^{k}
for all
k
iff
n=0
. Thus,
\eta

gives an injective ring map
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. We will usually suppress
notation for this and regard
\mathbb{Z}
as a subring of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Definition 12.7
.

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
we define
v(a)=\min\{k\;|\;\pi_{k}(a)\neq 0\}
(or

v(a)=\infty
if
\pi_{k}(a)=0
for all
k
, which means that
a=0
).
We also define
d(a,b)=p^{-v(a-b)}
, with the convention

p^{-\infty}=0
.

Proposition 12.8
.

The function
d
defines a metric on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(called the

p
-adic metric
), with respect to which it is complete and
compact. Moreover, the subspace
\mathbb{Z}
is dense.

Proof.

It is clear that
d(a,b)=d(b,a)
, and that this is nonnegative and
vanishes if and only if
a=b
. This just leaves the triangle
inequality
d(a,c)\leq d(a,b)+d(b,c)
. This is clear if
a=b
or

b=c
, so suppose that
a\neq b\neq c
. Put

m=\min(v(a-b),v(b-c))
. For
k<m
we have

\pi_{k}(a)=\pi_{k}(b)=\pi_{k}(c)
. It follows that
v(c-a)\geq m
, so

d(a,c)\leq p^{-m}=\max(d(a,b),d(b,c))\leq d(a,b)+d(b,c)

as required.

Now consider a Cauchy sequence
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\dotsc)
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
we can choose
m
such that
d(a_{i},a_{j})<p^{-k}
for
all
i,j\geq m
. From the definition of
d
, this means that the
element
\pi_{k}(a_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
is independent of
i
for
i\geq m
.
Let
b_{k}
denote this element. If
i
is large enough we will have

b_{k}=\pi_{k}(a_{i})
and also
b_{k+1}=\pi_{k+1}(a_{i})
; using this we see
that the projection
\mathbb{Z}/p^{k+1}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
sends
b_{k+1}
to
b_{k}
.
Thus, sequence
(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\dotsc)
is an element
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and
by construction
a_{i}\to b
as
i\to\infty
. This shows that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

is compact.

Next, suppose we are given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and we put

T_{k}=\{0,1,\dotsc,p^{k}-1\}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. The map
T_{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
is
a bijection, so for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
there is a unique
m\in T_{k}
with

\pi_{k}(m)=\pi_{k}(a)
, so
d(m,a)<p^{-k}
. Using this we see that
\mathbb{Z}

is dense in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Next, recall that an
\epsilon
-net
in a
metric space
X
is a finite set
F\subseteq X
such that every point is
within
\epsilon
of a point in
F
, that
X
is
totally bounded
if
it has an
\epsilon
-net for every
\epsilon>0
, and that a complete metric
space is compact if and only if it is totally bounded. The set
T_{k}

is a
2^{-k}
-net, so
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is totally bounded, so it is compact.
∎

Proposition 12.9
.

Put
D=\{0,1,2,\dotsc,p-1\}
(the set of
p
-adic digits). Then
there is a bijection

\sigma\colon\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}D\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}

given by
\sigma(u)=\sum_{i}u_{i}p^{i}
(a convergent sum with respect to the

p
-adic metric). In particular,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is uncountable. We call

\sigma^{-1}(a)
the
base
p
expansion
of
a
.

Proof.

It is elementary that the corresponding map

\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}D\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
is a bijection, and the claim follows
by passing to inverse limits.
∎

Proposition 12.10
.

The ring
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is torsion free and is an integral domain. It is
also a local ring, with
p.\mathbb{Z}_{p}
being the unique maximal ideal. The
group of units is

\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}=\{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\;|\;\pi_{0}(a)\neq 0\}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus p\mathbb{Z}_{p},

and every nonzero element is a unit times
p^{k}
for some
k
.

Proof.

First, consider an element
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with
\pi_{0}(a)=1
. We see from
Proposition
12.5
that
a=1-px
for some

x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. It follows easily that the series
\sum_{i}(px)^{i}
is
Cauchy, so it converges to some
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and we find that
ab=1
.
More generally, suppose merely that
\pi_{0}(a)\neq 0
in
\mathbb{Z}/p
. As

\mathbb{Z}/p
is a field, we can find
b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\pi_{0}(b)
is
inverse to
\pi_{0}(a)
. We then find that
ab
is invertible by the
previous case, and it follows that
a
is invertible. Conversely, as

\pi_{0}
is a ring map it certainly sends units to nonzero elements.
We therefore see that

\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}=\{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\;|\;\pi_{0}(a)\neq 0\}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus p\mathbb{Z}_{p}

as claimed. Note also that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is the field
\mathbb{Z}/p
, so

p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a maximal ideal. If
\mathfrak{m}
is any maximal ideal
we must have
\mathfrak{m}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}=\emptyset
, so

\mathfrak{m}\leq p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, so
\mathfrak{m}=p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
by maximality.
Thus,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a local ring.

Next, using base
p
expansions we see easily that multiplication by

p
is injective, and that every nonzero element
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
can be
written as
a=p^{k}b
for some
k\geq 0
and
b
with
\pi_{0}(b)\neq 0
,
so
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. As multiplication by
p^{k}
is injective and
b

is invertible we see that multiplication by
a
is injective. This
means that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is an integral domain. By considering

a\in\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}
we also see that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is torsion free.
∎

We can now understand the completion of free modules.

Definition 12.11
.

Let
I
be a set, and let
f
be a function from
I
to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. We
say that
f
is
asymptotically zero
if for all
k
, the set

\{i\;|\;v(f(i))<k\}
is finite. We write
AZ(I)
for the set of
asymptotically zero maps, which is a group under addition.

Proposition 12.12
.

The completion
\mathbb{Z}[I]_{p}
is naturally isomorphic to
AZ(I)
.

Proof.

First, we put

(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k})[I]=\{f\colon I\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}\;|\;\{i\;|\;f(i)\neq 0\}\text{ is finite }\}.

We write
\pi_{k}
for the projection
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
, or the projection

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
. We then write
\pi^{\prime}_{k}(f)=\pi_{k}\circ f
; this defines
a map
AZ(I)\to(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k})[I]
, which we can restrict to

\mathbb{Z}[I]\leq AZ(I)
. Note that
\pi^{\prime}_{k}(f)=0
iff
f(i)\in p^{k}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for
all
i
, in which case we can define
g=f/p^{k}\colon I\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and we find
that
g
is again asymptotically zero, so
f\in p^{k}AZ(I)
. This
shows that
\pi^{\prime}_{k}
induces an isomorphism

AZ(I)/p^{k}AZ(I)\to(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k})[I]
. By a similar argument, it also
induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}[I]/p^{k}\mathbb{Z}[I]\to(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k})[I]
. Thus, we
have
\mathbb{Z}[I]_{p}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k})[I]
. The maps

\pi^{\prime}_{k}\colon AZ(I)\to(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k})[I]
therefore assemble to give a
homomorphism
\pi^{\prime}\colon AZ(I)\to\mathbb{Z}[I]_{p}
. In the opposite direction,
suppose we have
g\in\mathbb{Z}[I]_{p}
. For each
k\geq 0
we therefore have

\pi_{k}(g)\in\mathbb{Z}[I]/p^{k}\mathbb{Z}[I]=(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k})[I]
, so there is a unique map

g_{k}\colon I\to\{0,1,\dotsc,p^{k}-1\}

with
\pi_{k}(g)(i)=g_{k}(i)\pmod{p^{k}}
for all
k
. Note that the set

\{i\;|\;g_{k}(i)\neq 0\}
is finite for all
k
. If we fix
i
, we
find that the sequence
\{g_{k}(i)\}_{k\geq 0}
is Cauchy, converging
to some element
g_{\infty}(i)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
say, and we have

g_{\infty}(i)=g_{k}(i)\pmod{p^{k}}
for all
k
. This means that

g_{\infty}\in AZ(I)
and
\pi^{\prime}(g_{\infty})=g
, so
\pi^{\prime}
is surjective.
We also have
\pi^{\prime}(h)=0
iff
h(i)
is divisible by
p^{k}
for all
i

and
k
, which implies that
h=0
. Thus, the map
\pi^{\prime}
is an
isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 12.13
.

For any abelian groups
A
and
B
there is a natural map

\mu\colon A_{p}\otimes B_{p}\to(A\otimes B)_{p}
, which induces an isomorphism

(A_{p}\otimes B_{p})_{p}\to(A\otimes B)_{p}
.

Proof.

Note that
p^{k}.1_{A\otimes B}=(p^{k}.1_{A})\otimes 1_{B}=1_{A}\otimes(p^{k}.1_{B})
. Using
the right exactness of tensor products we see that

(A\otimes B)/p^{k}=(A/p^{k})\otimes B=A\otimes(B/p^{k})=(A/p^{k})\otimes(B/p^{k}).

Combining this with Proposition
12.5

gives

(A_{p}\otimes B_{p})/p^{k}=(A_{p}/p^{k})\otimes(B_{p}/p^{k})=(A/p^{k})\otimes(B/p^{k})=(A\otimes B)/p^{k}.

Passing to inverse limits gives an isomorphism

(A_{p}\otimes B_{p})_{p}=(A\otimes B)_{p}
. We can compose this with the map

\eta\colon A_{p}\otimes B_{p}\to(A_{p}\otimes B_{p})_{p}
to get a map

\mu\colon A_{p}\otimes B_{p}\to(A\otimes B)_{p}
, which is what we most often need for
applications.
∎

Proposition 12.14
.

There is a natural map
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A\to A_{p}
, which is an isomorphism
when
A
is finitely generated.

Proof.

The map is just the composite
