\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A\xrightarrow{1\otimes\eta}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A_{p}\xrightarrow{\mu}(\mathbb{Z}\otimes A)_{p}=A_{p}

(or it can be defined more directly by the method used for
\mu
).
By the classification of finitely generated abelian groups, it will
suffice to prove that we have an isomorphism when
A=\mathbb{Z}
or

A=\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
or
A=\mathbb{Z}/q^{k}
for some prime
q\neq p
. The case
A=\mathbb{Z}

is clear. When
A=\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
we have
A_{p}=A
as in
Example
12.2
. We also
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A=\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{k}\mathbb{Z}_{p}

by the right exactness of tensoring, and this is the same as

\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}=A
by Proposition
12.5
, so

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A=A_{p}
as claimed. Finally, if
q
is different from
p

then
q^{k}
is invertible in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
so
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/q^{k}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}/q^{k}\mathbb{Z}_{p}=0
,
and similarly
(\mathbb{Z}/q^{k})_{p}=0
as in
Example
12.2
again.
∎

Corollary 12.15
.

If
A\to B\to C
is an exact sequence of finitely generated abelian
groups, then the resulting sequence
A_{p}\to B_{p}\to C_{p}
is also
exact.

Proof.

As
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is torsion free, Proposition
7.16
tells
us that the sequence
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes B\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes C
is exact.
∎

We can now assemble our results to prove something closely analogous
to Proposition
9.14
:

Proposition 12.16
.

(a)

The group
A_{p}
is always
p
-complete.

(b)

The map
\eta\colon A\to A_{p}
is an isomorphism if and
only if
A
is
p
-complete.

(c)

If
A
is
p
-complete then it can be regarded as a module
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

(d)

Suppose that
f\colon A\to B
is a homomorphism, and that
B

is
p
-complete. Then there is a unique homomorphism

f^{\prime}\colon A_{p}\to B
such that
f^{\prime}\circ\eta=f\colon A\to B
.

Proof.

(a)

As mentioned previously, this follows from
Proposition
12.5
by taking inverse
limits.

(b)

This is true by definition, and is only mentioned to
complete the correspondence with
Proposition
9.14
.

(c)

Proposition
12.13
gives a map

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes A_{p}\to(\mathbb{Z}\otimes A)_{p}=A_{p}=A.

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
a\in A
we can thus define
ra=\mu(r\otimes a)
.
Equivalently, this is characterised by the fact that

\pi_{k}(ra)=\pi_{k}(r)\pi_{k}(a)
in
A/p^{k}A
(where we have used the
obvious structure of
A/p^{k}A
as a module over
\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}
). From
this description it is clear that our multiplication rule is
associative, unital and distributive, so it makes
A
into a module
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

(d)

The map
f\colon A\to B
induces an isomorphism

f_{p}\colon A_{p}\to B_{p}
, and we have an isomorphism
\eta\colon B\to B_{p}
. We
can and must take
f^{\prime}=\eta^{-1}\circ f_{p}
.

∎

While our definition of completion is quite natural and
straightforward, its exactness properties for infinitely generated
groups are very delicate, and they do not relate well to topological
constructions. We will therefore introduce a different definition
that often agrees with completion, but has better formal properties.

Definition 12.17
.

For any abelian group
A
, we let
A[\![x]\!]
denote the group of
formal power series
v(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}x^{i}
with
a_{i}\in A

for all
i
. This is a module over
\mathbb{Z}[\![x]\!]
by the obvious rule

\left(\sum_{i}n_{i}x^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j}a_{j}x^{j}\right)=\sum_{k}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}n_{i}a_{k-i}\right)x^{k}.

We define

\displaystyle L_{0}A

\displaystyle=A[\![x]\!]/((x-p).A[\![x]\!])

\displaystyle L_{1}A

\displaystyle=\{v(x)\in A[\![x]\!]\;|\;(x-p)v(x)=0\}.

We can identify
A
with the set of constant series in
A[\![x]\!]
,
and then restrict the quotient map
A[\![x]\!]\to L_{0}A
to
A
to get a
natural map
\eta\colon A\to L_{0}A
. We say that
A
is

Ext-
p
-complete
if
\eta\colon A\to L_{0}A
is an isomorphism and

L_{1}A=0
. We also call
L_{0}A
the
derived completion
of
A
.

Remark 12.18
.

Readers familiar with the general theory of derived functors should
consult Corollary
12.27
to see why the term is
appropriate here.

Remark 12.19
.

It will follow from Proposition
12.28
that the
condition
L_{1}A=0
is actually automatic when
\eta\colon A\to L_{0}A
is an
isomorphism. However, it is easier to develop the theory if we have
both conditions in the initial definition.

Remark 12.20
.

There is an evident product map

\mu\colon A[\![x]\!]\otimes B[\![x]\!]\to(A\otimes B)[\![x]\!]

given by

\mu\left(\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}x^{i}\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{j}b_{j}x^{j}\right)\right)=\sum_{k}\left(\sum_{k=i+j}a_{i}\otimes b_{j}\right)x^{k}.

This induces a map
\mu\colon(L_{0}A)\otimes(L_{0}B)\to L_{0}(A\otimes B)
, which fits
in a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L_{0}A)\otimes(L_{0}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}(A\otimes B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Remark 12.21
.

If we have a short exact sequence
A\xrightarrow{}B\xrightarrow{}C
, we can apply the
Snake Lemma to the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[\![x]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[\![x]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C[\![x]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[\![x]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[\![x]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C[\![x]\!]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x-p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x-p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x-p}

to obtain an exact sequence

L_{1}A\rightarrowtail L_{1}B\to L_{1}C\to L_{0}A\to L_{0}B\twoheadrightarrow L_{0}C.

Proposition 12.22
.

(a)

If we have a short exact sequence
A\to B\to C
in which
two of the three terms are Ext-
p
-complete, then so is the third.

(b)

Finite sums and retracts of Ext-
p
-complete groups are
Ext-
p
-complete.

(c)

The kernel, cokernel and image of any homomorphism
between Ext-
p
-complete groups are Ext-
p
-complete.

(d)

The product of any (possibly infinite) family of
Ext-
p
-complete groups is Ext-
p
-complete.

(e)

If
p^{k}.1_{A}=0
for some
k
then
A
is
Ext-
p
-complete.

(f)

If
A
is
p
-complete then it is Ext-
p
-complete.

Proof.

(a)

Chase the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}C}

(b)

Clear.

(c)

Consider a homomorphism
f\colon A\to B

between Ext-
p
-complete groups, and the resulting short exact
sequences
\operatorname{img}(f)\to B\to\operatorname{cok}(f)
and
\ker(f)\to A\to\operatorname{img}(f)
.
These give diagrams
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{img}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cok}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}\operatorname{img}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}\operatorname{cok}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}\operatorname{img}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}\operatorname{cok}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{img}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}\ker(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}\operatorname{img}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}\ker(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}\operatorname{img}(f).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

From the first diagram we see that
L_{1}\operatorname{img}(f)=0
and that the map

\operatorname{img}(f)\to L_{0}\operatorname{img}(f)
is injective, and from the second we see that
the map
\operatorname{img}(f)\to L_{0}\operatorname{img}(f)
is surjective; thus
\operatorname{img}(f)
is
Ext-
p
-complete. Given this, it is a special case of (a) that

\ker(f)
and
\operatorname{cok}(f)
are also Ext-
p
-complete as claimed.

(d)

This is clear, because

(\prod_{i}A_{i})[\![x]\!]=\prod_{i}A_{i}[\![x]\!]
.

(e)

If
k=1
the definitions give

L_{0}A=A[\![x]\!]/(x.A[\![x]\!])=A
and

L_{1}A=\{v(x)\in A[\![x]\!]\;|\;xv(x)=0\}=0
as required. The general
case follows by induction using (a) and the short exact sequence

pA\to A\to A/pA
.

(f)

As the tower
\{A/p^{k}\}
consists of surjections, the

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}
term is zero. As
A
is
p
-complete, we therefore
have a short exact sequence

A\rightarrowtail\prod_{k}A/p^{k}\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{k}A/p^{k}.

The second and third terms are Ext-
p
-complete by parts (e)
and (d), so
A
is Ext-
p
-complete by part (a).

∎

Proposition 12.23
.

Let
A
be any abelian group. Then
L_{1}A=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}A[p^{k}]
and there
is a natural short exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}^{1}A[p^{k}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{p}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

The limit symbols here refer to the tower

0=A[p^{0}]\xleftarrow{p}A[p]\xleftarrow{p}A[p^{2}]\xleftarrow{p}A[p^{3}]\xleftarrow{p}\dotsb

Proof.

First, an element of
L_{1}A
is a series
v(x)=\sum_{i}a_{i}x^{i}
with

(x-p)v(x)=0
, which means that
pa_{0}=0
and
pa_{i+1}=a_{i}
for all

i\geq 0
. It follows inductively that
p^{i+1}a_{i}=0
for all
i
,
so the sequence
(0,a_{0},a_{1},\dotsc)
is an element of

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A[p^{i}]
. All steps here can be reversed so

L_{1}A=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A[p^{i}]
. Next, define
\zeta^{\prime}_{i}\colon A[\![x]\!]\to A/p^{i}A
by

\zeta^{\prime}_{i}(\sum_{j}a_{j}x^{j})=\sum_{j<i}a_{j}p^{j}+p^{i}A.

It is clear that
\zeta^{\prime}_{i}(v(x))=\zeta^{\prime}_{i+1}(v(x))\pmod{p^{i}A}
, so the
maps
\zeta^{\prime}_{i}
fit together to give a homomorphism

\zeta^{\prime}\colon A[\![x]\!]\to A_{p}
, which can be described heuristically as

\zeta^{\prime}(v(x))=v(p)
. It is also easy to check that
\zeta^{\prime}((x-p)w(x))=0

for all
w(x)
, so there is an induced map
\zeta\colon L_{0}A\to A_{p}
. Given
an arbitrary element
b\in A_{p}
we can choose
b_{i}\in A
representing
the coset
\pi_{i}(b)\in A/p^{i}A
. These will then satisfy

b_{i+1}=b_{i}\pmod{p^{i}A}
, so we can choose
a_{i}\in A
with

b_{i+1}=p^{i}a_{i}+b_{i}
. The series
v(x)=\sum_{i}a_{i}x^{i}
then has

\zeta(v(x))=b
, so we see that
\zeta
is surjective.

Next, for any
c\in\prod_{i}A[p^{i}]
put

\xi^{\prime\prime}(c)=\sum_{i}c_{i}x^{i}\in A[\![x]\!]
, and let
\xi^{\prime}(c)
denote the image
of
\xi^{\prime\prime}(c)
in
L_{0}A
. It is clear by construction that
\zeta\xi^{\prime}=0
.
Suppose that
c
lies in the image of the map

D\colon\prod_{i}A[p^{i}]\to\prod_{i}A[p^{i}]
, so there is a sequence

(d_{i})_{i\geq 0}
with
p^{i}d_{i}=0
and
c_{i}=d_{i}-pd_{i+1}
for all

i
. Note that
d_{0}=p^{0}d_{0}=0
and put
w(x)=\sum_{i}d_{i+1}x^{i}
; we
find that
\xi^{\prime\prime}(c)=(x-p)w(x)
and so
\xi^{\prime}(c)=0
. We thus have an
induced map
\xi\colon\operatorname{cok}(D)=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A[p^{i}]\to L_{0}A
with
\zeta\xi=0
.
Consider an arbitrary element
v(x)=\sum_{i}a_{i}x^{i}\in A[\![x]\!]
with

\zeta^{\prime}(v(x))=0
. This means that for all
i\geq 0
we can choose

b_{i}\in A
with
\sum_{j<i}a_{j}p^{j}=p^{i}b_{i}
. It follows that
b_{0}=0

and
p^{i}b_{i}+p^{i}a_{i}=p^{i+1}b_{i+1}
, so the element

c_{i}=b_{i}+a_{i}-pb_{i+1}
has
p^{i}c_{i}=0
, so
c\in\prod_{i}A[p^{i}]
. If we
put
w(x)=\sum_{i}b_{i+1}x^{i}
we find that

v(x)=\xi^{\prime\prime}(c)-(x-p)w(x),

so in
L_{0}A
we have
v=\xi(c)
. This proves that the map

\xi\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A[p^{i}]\to\ker(\zeta)
is surjective.

Finally, suppose we have
c\in\prod_{i}A[p^{i}]
with
\xi^{\prime}(c)=0
. This
means that there exists
u(x)=\sum_{i}b_{i}x^{i}\in A[\![x]\!]
with

\xi^{\prime\prime}(c)=(x-p)u(x)
, so
c_{0}=-pb_{0}
and
c_{i+1}=b_{i}-pb_{i+1}
for
all
i\geq 0
. Form this it follows easily that
p^{i+1}b_{i}=0
for
all
i\geq 0
, so we have an element

b^{\prime}=(0,b_{0},b_{1},\dotsc)\in\prod_{i}A[p^{i}]
. We now see that
c=D(b^{\prime})
,
so
c
represents the zero element of
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A[p^{i}]
, thus, the
map
\xi\colon\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{i}A[p^{i}]\to\ker(\zeta)
is injective.
∎

Remark 12.24
.

It is clear from the definitions that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

commutes.

Definition 12.25
.

We say that an abelian group
A
has
bounded
p
-torsion
if
there exists
k\geq 0
such that
p^{k}.\operatorname{tors}_{p}(A)=0
.

Corollary 12.26
.

If
A
has bounded
p
-torsion (in particular, if
A
is a free
abelian group) then
L_{1}A=0
and
L_{0}A=A_{p}
.

Proof.

The tower
\{A[p^{i}]\}
is nilpotent, so

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}A[p^{i}]=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{i}^{1}A[p^{i}]=0
by
Proposition
11.13
.
∎

Corollary 12.27
.

Suppose we have a short exact sequence
P\xrightarrow{f}Q\to A
where
P
and

Q
are free abelian groups. Then
L_{0}A
and
L_{1}A
are the cokernel
and kernel of the induced map
P_{p}\to Q_{p}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from Corollary
12.26
and
Remark
12.21
.
∎

Proposition 12.28
.

For any abelian group
A
, the groups
L_{0}A
,
L_{1}A
,
A_{p}
and

\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}^{1}A[p^{k}]
are all Ext-
p
-complete.

Proof.

The groups
A[p^{k}]
and
A/p^{k}
are Ext-
p
-complete by part (e) of
Proposition
12.22
. It follows by part (d) that

\prod_{k}A[p^{k}]
and
\prod_{k}A/p^{k}
are Ext-
p
-complete, and then by
part (c) that the groups
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}A[p^{k}]=L_{1}A
,
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{k}A[p^{k}]

and
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}A/p^{k}=A_{p}
are Ext-
p
-complete. We can thus apply
part (a) to the short exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}^{1}A[p^{k}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

to deduce that
L_{0}A
is Ext-
p
-complete.
∎

Proposition 12.29
.

For any abelian group
A
, we have
L_{0}A=0
iff
A_{p}=0
iff
A/p=0
.

Proof.

Proposition
12.23
shows that
A_{p}
is a quotient of
L_{0}A
,
and Proposition
12.5
shows that
A/p
is
a quotient of
A_{p}
. Conversely, if
A/p=0
then
p.1_{A}
is
surjective, so
A/p^{k}=0
for all
k
and the maps in the tower

\{A[p^{k}]\}
are all surjective. It follows that

A_{p}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}_{k}A/p^{k}=0
and (using
Proposition
11.17
) that
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\longleftarrow}{lim}}^{1}_{k}A[p^{k}]=0
, so
the short exact sequence in Proposition
12.23
shows that

L_{0}A=0
.
∎

We next explain a more traditional construction of the functors
L_{0}

and
L_{1}
. This involves a group known as
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}
.

Definition 12.30
.

Define
f_{k}\colon\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{k+1}
by
f_{k}(a+p^{k}\mathbb{Z})=pa+p^{k+1}\mathbb{Z}
, so
we have a sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0=\mathbb{Z}/p^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{4}}

We define
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}
to be the colimit of this sequence.

Proposition 12.31
.

There are canonical isomorphisms

\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}=\mathbb{Z}[1/p]/\mathbb{Z}=\operatorname{tors}_{p}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})=({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})_{(p)}={\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}.

Proof.

First, consider the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/p^{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{4}}

Using Propositions
10.10

and
10.8
we obtain a short exact sequence

\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[1/p]\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}
, so
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}=\mathbb{Z}[1/p]/\mathbb{Z}
. Next,
for
a\in{\mathbb{Q}}
we note that
a+\mathbb{Z}
is a
p
-torsion element in
{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}}

iff
p^{k}a\in\mathbb{Z}
for some
k
, iff
a\in\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
. It follows that

\operatorname{tors}_{p}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})=\mathbb{Z}[1/p]/\mathbb{Z}
. We also know from
Proposition
9.17
that

\operatorname{tors}_{p}({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})=({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})_{(p)}
. It is clear that
{\mathbb{Q}}
is
p
-local, so

{\mathbb{Q}}_{(p)}={\mathbb{Q}}
. We can thus apply
Proposition
9.10
to the sequence

\mathbb{Z}\rightarrowtail{\mathbb{Q}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}}
to see that
({\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}})_{(p)}={\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
.
∎

Proposition 12.32
.

There are natural isomorphisms
L_{0}A=\operatorname{Ext}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty},A)
and

L_{1}A=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty},A)
.

Proof.

In this proof we will identify
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}
with
\mathbb{Z}[{\textstyle\frac{1}{p}}]/\mathbb{Z}
. Put

F=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}
, and let
e_{i}
be the
i
’th basis
vector in
F
. Define maps

F\xrightarrow{\phi}F\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}

by

\phi(e_{i})=e_{i-1}-pe_{i}\hskip 60.00009pt\psi(e_{i})=p^{-i-1}+\mathbb{Z}

(where
e_{-1}
is interpreted as
0
), or equivalently

\displaystyle\phi(n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},\dotsc)

\displaystyle=(pn_{0}-n_{1},pn_{1}-n_{2},pn_{2}-n_{3},\dotsc)

\displaystyle\phi(m_{0},m_{1},m_{2},\dotsc)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}m_{i}p^{-i-1}+\mathbb{Z}.

By considering the first nonzero entry in
n=(n_{0},n_{1},\dotsc)
, we
see that
\phi
is injective. Any element of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}
can be written
as
k/p^{i+1}+\mathbb{Z}
for some
i\geq 0
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and this is the
same as
\psi(ke_{i})
, so
\psi
is surjective. It is clear from the
definitions that
\psi\phi=0
, so
\operatorname{img}(\phi)\leq\ker(\psi)
.
Conversely, suppose we have
n\in F
with
\psi(n)=0
, so the number

q=\sum_{i}n_{i}p^{-1-i}
actually lies in
\mathbb{Z}
. Put

m_{i}=\sum_{j<i}n_{j}p^{i-j-1}-p^{i}q
, and note that
m_{i}=0
for

i\gg 0
, so
m\in F
. We find that
\phi(m)=n
, so the sequence

F\xrightarrow{\phi}F\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}
is short exact. As
F
is free, this
gives us an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}(F,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}(F,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty},A).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}

Next, for any series
v(x)=\sum_{i}a_{i}x^{i}\in A[\![x]\!]
we have a
homomorphism
\alpha(v(x))\colon F\to A
given by
\alpha(v(x))(e_{i})=a_{i}
for
all
i\geq 0
. This construction gives an isomorphism

A[\![x]\!]\to\operatorname{Hom}(F,A)
. If we make the conventions
a_{-1}=0
and

e_{-1}=0
we also have

\alpha((x-p)v(x))(e_{j})=\alpha\left(\sum_{i}(a_{i-1}-pa_{i})x^{i}\right)(e_{j})=a_{j-1}-pa_{j}=\alpha(v(x))(e_{j-1}-pe_{j})=\alpha(v(x))(\phi(e_{j}))

Thus, multiplication by
x-p
on
A[\![x]\!]
corresponds to
\phi^{*}

on
\operatorname{Hom}(F,A)
, and the claim follows from this.
∎
